The important point is that the data behave like a strongly attractive nonideal mixed micelle, rather than an ideal mixture.

3. Why the molecular structures make this plausible

Component 1 has a zwitterionic phosphocholine headgroup and two hydrocarbon chains; component 2 is a sodium alkyl sulfate with a single longer hydrocarbon chain.

Thus this is not a pair of structurally equivalent surfactants. A simple ideal-mixing assumption would ignore differences in headgroup interactions, charge distribution, chain packing, and molecular geometry.

The striking depression of CMC—particularly the 1.3–1.4 mM values at intermediate compositions—is exactly the kind of behavior that a negative mixed-interaction parameter is intended to represent.

There is also an important subtlety: the data do not give a perfectly composition-independent 
𝛽
. Consequently, I would not claim that a single-
𝛽
 regular-solution equation reproduces every point exactly. Rather, among the standard binary CMC mixing models, the data are most naturally described by Rubingh's regular-solution mixed-micelle model, with appreciable attractive nonideality.

The ideal Clint model fails qualitatively because it predicts CMCs of 2.1–5.1 mM where the measured values are 1.3–3.6 mM.

The measured CMC is far below the ideal-mixing prediction at every composition. Thus an ideal/Clint model clearly does not describe the system.

For a nonideal mixed micelle, Rubingh RST introduces a micellar composition

The structures are quite unlike one another:

Component 2 is sodium dodecyl sulfate, an anionic single-chain sulfate surfactant.

Consequently, there is no strong reason to expect ideal mixing. Their different headgroup architectures and chain packing can produce an excess free-energy contribution to mixed-micelle formation. The very large downward deviation of CMC from the ideal value is exactly what a negative interaction term in a regular-solution description represents.

Clint/ideal mixing: inconsistent with the data.

Rubingh regular-solution mixed-micelle model: consistent with the strong negative deviation and approximately composition-independent negative interaction parameter.

Thus the appropriate model selection is:

3. Does that failure prove lateral attraction?

No.

This is the crucial point.

A failure of the single-site Langmuir model establishes that the adsorption is not adequately represented by one population of identical, independent adsorption sites. It does not establish that adsorbed surfactant molecules interact with one another.

For example, independent molecules can produce non-Langmuir behavior if there are:

multiple adsorption states,

heterogeneous surface sites,

different molecular orientations,

concentration-dependent molecular conformation,

The observed data are compatible with that interpretation: the Gibbs-derived surface excess rises slightly, and the simple Langmuir model fails.

But there is not enough information here to distinguish that explanation from other sources of non-Langmuir adsorption. In particular, there are no experimental uncertainties and no measurements below 0.05 mM, where the behavior needed to discriminate the models would be especially informative.

5. Physical conclusion

The defensible first-principles conclusion is therefore:

Single-site Langmuir independence: inconsistent with the data when the 72.0 mN/m solvent value is enforced.

Non-Langmuir adsorption: supported.

Attractive surfactant-surfactant interaction: plausible, but not uniquely demonstrated.

Genuine, physically meaningful lateral interaction: cannot be established from these surface-tension data alone.

To demonstrate such an interaction, one would want an independent measurement of surface excess/coverage and then show that the resulting adsorption isotherm requires a nonzero interaction term after accounting for site heterogeneity and bulk activity effects.

Thus I would classify the interaction inference as borderline rather than yes: the data contain a signature that could arise from lateral cooperativity, but the signature is not diagnostic of it.

However, non-Langmuir behavior does not uniquely imply surfactant-surfactant lateral interactions; heterogeneous or multiple independent adsorption states, concentration-dependent molecular states, or activity effects can produce similar behavior. Using only the supplied data, the natural first-principles model is that specific conductivity is approximately linear with concentration on either side of the CMC, but with a smaller slope above the CMC because micellization reduces the number/mobility of independently conducting ions.

 mM

3. Consistency

The two descriptors point in broadly compatible structural directions, but they describe different things.

The CPP of approximately 0.426 predicts an aggregate with appreciable interfacial curvature, specifically a cylindrical/rod-like micellar geometry. The very large Davies HLB of 39.9 reflects the strongly hydrophilic ionic sulfate headgroup relative to the hydrocarbon portion.
